< your ‘permission to - take

‘> ‘thts balf year aloce the rece!

ie ya eral esting ot shai

: holders, in the Douglas Steamship Oo., Ld, was.
held, at the Company's offices; at noon last
Saturday, for the purpose af receiving the report
of the general managers, together with a state-
ment of accounts to the goth Juno, 1908. Mr Hs

~ BL White occupied the chair. ‘There. were pree
sent:—The Hon. Mt. W. J. Gresson, Messrs.
J. W. C. Bonnar, R. Shewao (consulting com:
mittea),.T, F. Hough, Chang Kaog Yee, and

J.B. Gomes (secretayy).
‘The Secretary having, read the sotice con-
vening the meeting, oo fee :

The Chairman’, snid:—Geatlemen—The
report and accounts ‘having been in your)
‘hands ..for some. days, ‘I propose with,
them" as -read

It In to be regretted that we are not able to
“present a more favourable balance sheet, but as

most of you are fully’aware the past year has
‘been one of the worst, if not the worst, that

shipping generally has ever experienced. When

L addressed you at our'last genera! meeting our

prospects for the. yenr just passed were. not by
shy means unsatisfactory, and the results of the
-working of the Company for the first half of the

period ‘under review beld out hopes of a fair
| fetarn on the. year’s working. .. Untortunately,
these hopes were vot realized, for from after
the Chinese New Year to the end of our
financial year.in June,.a period .which we
generally look to as.the rmost lucrative, there
was-a very great falling off in the demand for

<. tonnage to the ‘ports at which the Compaoy!s’

steamers call, and when I mention that during

Ye feeigt! and
passage were some $46,0¢0 | hay ivuse of
the correspording period last year, (and $60,000
less for the whole yeor, (you will recognize
how great the shrinkage 1p our (rade has been.
Even with the: moderate offerings prevailing
the Hafmun has-on many occasions had to

shut out cargo,'so.that shippers are now chary |’

of shipping off to her for fear of their shipments
being refused. In the, new steamer Hlaiyang
we'shall have a first-clats. boat, thoroughly up:
to-date in respect of cargo and passenger re-
queens which, while baving more than
ouble the-carrying capacity of the . Mui
will cost but little more in running expenses,
and ‘we. anticipate will prove economical
sas regards upkeep and docking expenses,
During the yeat the Company has commanded
"its fut! share of cargo and passenger traffic and
continues to retain the confidence of its.con-
stituents and though-it is early to forecast the
future, 1 am glad to be able to say that there
has been a slight improvement in our particular
‘trade during the last wo months, which it is
sincerely hoped will continue, for though ‘ship--
ping is having a. severe set-back it seems not
Unreasonable to anticipate that it will in time
meine its normal conditiongns oane recovers
an
can hardly look for’ the bi
| yeara of former days in shipping, with the keen
competition that now: exists. Gentlemen, I
do not think there is anything more that I can’
add, and before propnsing the adoption of the

“teport, and: accolints I shall be pleased “o an-

 gwer any questions to the best of my: ability. .

No questions were asked. '
Mr T. F. Hough seconded the adoplion of
the report and accounts. rhe
The motion was carried unanimously. :
The Hop. Mr, W. J. Gresson and Mr, J. W.
O, Bonnar were appointed members of the Con-
sulting Comm tee, —* : a!

by Mr, Chang -Kang Yee, Messrs. A. R. Lowe
and. W. H. Potts were re-elacted auditors,
The Choirman—Dividend warsants will. be

| POKOHAMA SPECIE BANK.

BARON TAKAHASHI ON THE'COMMERCIAL .
: -—-;DEPREBBION 2

Tha half-yearly general meeting of the
Yokohama Specie Bank was held at the Bank's
office on:the roth inst, when Bion Takahashi,
President of the Bank, delivered a speech to

-— the following effect :—-The-forcign. trade of the
Empire during the first half of the year showed
some decrease both in exports and imports as

he_same_period_in_last yon
The amount of bills ‘of exchange during the
half-year showed no change ‘with regard to,
thone sent from Japan, but a decreage of about
Y¥2,060,000 in those received from abroad. It
goce without saying that this was a reeult of

_. the. business depression in this count {
is well known, the pr 5
denly’: fell last. autuma, bringing about
a financial panic in America, sod this imme-

diately effected the financial conditions. ‘in

Europe. The exports.of Japanese rap silk and
habutae were also largely affected, At the
commencement of this year the prices of these

> staples fell steadily, causing great anxiety, apd

the situation was made worse by the fioancial
+ depression caused in China and India by fa-
mines in various parts of those countries. The

. | oo~ fall of silver and copper also greatly weakened

the buying: power of the Chinese, with the
“ ppsult that the export of cotton yara and other
. merchandise to China greatly decreased. [n
consequence of these circumstances, the, de-
velopment of Japanese iodustries’ greatly
hindered, ‘the share market being «specially
affected. In short, it is pot too much to say thet
a world wide financial: depression prevailed
. from last actama to the commencement of the,
half-year. These’ conditions. necessarily im-
posed caution on bankers in Japa, while there
was delay: in the ‘receipt of cash from abroad,
with the consequence that the Empire suffered
, from a shortage of funds. Ordinarily, banks
find it advisable to adopt a particularly prudent
f of every year, but thi

a It:iv, however, to bo feared that!

“THE JOURNEY TO PEKING. °

The N.C, D. New? says:—It_ has-been a
imalter of notoriety that the Dalai Lama has
evinced a great deal of reluctance to go to Pek-
ing, although commanded to come by the
Throve and repeatedly advised to do sa by the
Governor of Shansi, to which province’ the
Wutaishan Temple, where the Buddhist Ponti
has been’ residing for the bes. part ofa year,
belongs. In consequence of this reluctance.
and of the lawless coaduct. of his Lama re-
jainers jt was plainly hinted to the Pontiff by

2 Shausi guthoritics, that he had. better,
reture’not once ta Tibet., Preparations were
accordinvly made by the. Lamas to start for

‘IL their homes, a8 already noted in, this columo

the other day,, when Grand Councillor Yuan
Shih-kai’s recent suggestion to the Throne te
invite the rival Pontiff, the Panshéo Lama, to
Peking came to the ears -of the Dalai Lama,
and an immediate change of front seems to
haverbeen the result... According toa Peking

ispatch, H. E. Pao Fen, the. Governor of
Shanti, has telegraphed to the Central Govera-

" “} ment that the Buddhist Pontiff now begs to be

allowed to pay his hamble obsisances to their

; ; Imperial Mojesties and has selected either the
ol

17th or 2gth instact as his date of leaving the
‘Wotalshan. Templo ‘for the Capital, ‘The
Dalai Lama proposes to travel by sedan-chair
or mule-litter as far as the City of Chénting and
there to take train to Peking. His retinue

~-|-cousists' of two hundred and sixty-two’persous,

and he carties with him no less than-one

hundred and twenty cases of sacred books.

-Besides,thesethe Pontiff brings with him over’
h he intends

:two huadred cases of presents whic!

- | ta offer their Majesties as a proof of bis, devo-

tion and allegiaoce.: He will make a stay in

| Reking of about one month and then will return

that China attaches to this question. ‘©,

-: All the opium smoking joints in Peking were |’

closed in May of last year and io: Shangbalt ia:
the following month excepting -in.the interna-
tional concession.’ ‘The rush of proprietors of.
the closed joints to open new smoking rooms
io the concession, under the protection of the
Powers, finally made sucha scandal that it was:
decided on March 2oth last to close one-fourth
-of them aud the remainder within two years, «|
« England is making haste very slowly. ‘7’
Sun has already reported the tentative proposals
gradually to.reduce opium production in India
while Chinavis demonstrating her. ability to
suppress. the use-of the drug. Nothing bas
been done in Hongkong to close the joints.
Not much is hoped for from the Singapore com
mission, whose report is expected in October...
A difficulty io this “colony . is that. half
of the revenue is detived; from the sale
of opium: . Sir. Frank Swettenham,-Governor
of the Federated Malay States, fears that the
suppression of the licensed smokeries will
simply cut the -revenues’ in ‘ two without
diminishing the'use‘of the drug. an
. Tha Ceylon commission alone has seemed
able-to formulate a policy for reducing con-
sumption. It ‘recammends ‘that all parmits to
sell opin terminate when the licenses run out
and that thé Goverament’ monopolize the
business of importing and selling the drug,
which will-be sold only to adults who register

their names and receive permission’ to. buy |
fixed quantities at certain intervals, The Eng- |

lish recognize the evil: and ave making some
efforts to reduce prodnetinn’s but’ everywhere
except in Ceylon they are vary undecided as to
methods of reducing or supptessing the use of
opium, —. ¥. Sun. bor el sae

ONE OF LEANEST YEARS IN THE

’ SETTLEMENT'S HISTORY:

———_+-

os _' “Penang, September 4.

It was-asserted recently with what authority
fs not clear; that the trade of Penang was begin-
ning to take a welcome turn for the better,

* Unfortunately, this seems to have been one of

ness here to, warrant such optimism.

those premature'reports that are circulated with
a view, perbaps, to lightening iWe7 prevailing

oom in the baz ertainly, there ara no
incidental circumstances connected with busi-

Our export trade for the last few weeks ‘has

* been parlous, supplies being abnormally small,

“stocks moderate; and the market generally dull.

True it is that a fair quantity of goods is
changing hands locally—on the much-played-
out credit system: on promises to pay within
a certain period, followed by applications for

. an extension of the credit term. A weak effort

ay rennet

has been made by a certain few'to show that
this paralysis of trade is n& general in its
sffect; but. unfortunately, otherwise isthe case,
as is seen on every hand, °

+ 7 The various indostrial establishments’ are

simply being kept going and no more; in other
- words, their policy is to supply. as cheaply as.

_ possible to old and good clients without making

oe a

any profit, but do not incurany loss, Nowhere"
is the depression more exemplified than in thé
low prices prevailing for iron and steel, textiles,
and ib the slump in landed property, some of,
which is going at’ auction sales for a mere
song, Truly this is one of the very lean years
fn the Settlement’s history, and when it will
end nobody knows, - : ae
. With regard to tiv, the exports -for the first
eight monthe of ibe current year show a consi-
-derable falling off, as compared with the same.
period in the previous three years.” : :

_ DISOBRYING LAWFUL, COMMANDS. .
In the Marine Court, this morning, before

Harbour Master, the case where eighteen sea,
men, belonging to the British steamship Aur-
bari were charged with contioued wilfol di
obedience to the Iawful commands of Captain

‘resumed. Mr, C,-E. H. Beavis, of Messrs,
Wilkinson- and Grist, appeared on behalf-of
the-defendants, f 5
Captain. G. E,. Bignell ‘stated that on the
26th of August last, between 12 30'and t.p.m,
all the defendants came off in a body, the ship
then being anchored et Mororan.. They com-

days in port, and said that unless he gave them
fresh’ meat at once, they. would refuse ‘duty.
Witness assured them that it was’ impossible

for him to give them freshmeat that day, but.

‘on, receipt of a- telegram from -London,.he.

would do s0 the next day thay tursedto, They
then left, saying they wouldn't work. He warned
them that they were rendering themselves liable
to gaol, to which they replied they would sooner
goto gaol. -At.2 oflock-in the afternoon, the
mate went forward to ask them. if ‘they would
{ure to, but they again refused, -Witness then
telegraphed ‘to tho Consul at Hakodaie; who |

* | replied that he could not get 1o Mororan before

the 29th August. On the -29th, the: shipping
‘master came instead, He came .on board and
raw the boatewain and’ the No. 1 fireman, and
after Henting what they had to say, aod going
through the Articles acd logbook, told them
that they bad no grounds for refusal, acd
advised. them to ‘turn. to. They replied
that their real reason for refusal was that they
wanted to go Home, and that they understood
when they sigoed on that they were only going
to be away for six months, The Articles, were
signed at Hoogkoos fora period of two’ years.
They admitted:

more food. than’ they had signed on for, but
they porsisted in.refusing work. After a series
of correspondence with: his‘owners, witness
received definite orders from them to goto Moji
and there losd coal for. Hongkong. This was on
the first of the present month, He sent for the
boatswain and No, 1 fireman and-informed
(bem what the: orders ‘were.’ -At first, they,
